Paralinearization and extended lifespan for solutions of the α \alpha -SQG sharp front equation
Massimiliano Berti, Scipio Cuccagna, Francisco Gancedo, Stefano Scrobogna

Abstract
In this paper we paralinearize the contour dynamics equation for sharp-fronts of α \alpha -SQG, for any α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) ∪ ( 1 , 2 ) \alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,2) , close to a circular vortex. This turns out to be a quasi-linear Hamiltonian PDE. After deriving the asymptotic expansion of the linear frequencies of oscillations at the vortex disk and verifying the absence of three wave interactions, we prove that, in the most singular cases α ∈ ( 1 , 2 ) \alpha\in(1,2) , any initial vortex patch which is ε \varepsilon -close to the disk exists for a time interval of size at least ∼ ε − 2 \sim\varepsilon^{-2} . This quadratic lifespan result relies on a paradifferential Birkhoff normal form reduction and exploits cancellations arising from the Hamiltonian nature of the equation. This is the first normal form long time existence result of sharp fronts.



stix@largesymbols"0E
stix@largesymbols"0F

Keywords:

\alpha
-SQG equations, vortex patches, paradifferential calculus,
Birkhoff normal form.

1
Introduction and main results

In this paper we consider the generalized surface
quasi-geostrophic
\alpha
-SQG equations

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\theta(t,\zeta)+u(t,\zeta)\cdot\nabla\theta(t,\zeta)=0\,,\quad\left(t,\zeta\right)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\,,

(1.1)

with velocity field

u\vcentcolon=\nabla^{\perp}\left|D\right|^{-2+\alpha}\theta\,,\qquad\left|D\right|\vcentcolon=(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,,\qquad\alpha\in(0,2)\,.

(1.2)

These class of
active scalar equations have been introduced in
[
68
,
28
]
and,
for
\alpha\to 0
,
formally reduce to the 2D-Euler equation in vorticity formulation
(in this case
\theta
is the vorticity of the fluid).
The case
\alpha=1

is the surface quasi-geostrophic (SQG) equation
in
[
23
]
which models the evolution of
the temperature
\theta
for atmospheric and oceanic flows.

For the 2D Euler equation global-in-time well-posedness
results are well known for either regular initial data,
see e.g.
[
65
,
22
]
,
as well as for

L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}
initial vorticities, thanks to the celebrated
Yudovich Theorem
[
77
]
.
This result is based on the fact that the vorticity is
transported by the particles of fluid
along the velocity field,
which turns out to be log-Lipschitz, and thus it defines a global flow on the plane.
On the other hand, for
\alpha>0
, an analogous result does not hold
because the velocity field
u
in (
1.2
) is more singular
and does not define a flow. Nevertheless
local in time smooth solutions exist
thanks to a nonlinear commutator structure of the vector field
for
\alpha=1
,
in

[
24
]
, and for
\alpha\in\left(1,2\right)
, in
[
21
]
. For
\alpha=1
, the works
[
27
,
26
]
rule out the possible formation of certain
kind of singularities but
the question of whether a finite-time singularity may develop from a smooth initial datum remains open. In this context we mention the construction in

[
45
]

of solutions
that must either exhibit infinite in time growth of derivatives or blow up in finite time.

Existence of global weak
L^{p}
solutions has been obtained by energy methods
for
\alpha=1
, if
p>4/3
, in
[
70
,
66
]
,
and for
\alpha\in\left(1,2\right)
if
p=2
, in
[
21
]
.
For
\alpha\in(0,1]
global weak solutions exist
also in
L^{1}\cap L^{2}
as proved in
[
63
]
.
We also mention that non-unique weak solutions of SQG
have been constructed by convex integration techniques in
[
14
,
58
]
.

A particular type of weak solutions are the

vortex
patches
-also called
sharp fronts
-
which are given by the characteristic function of an evolving domain

\theta\left(t,\zeta\right)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}1\quad\text{if }\zeta\in D(t)\,,\\
0\quad\text{if }\zeta\notin D(t)\,.\end{cases}\qquad D(t)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\,.

(1.3)

The vortex patch
problem (
1.3
)
can be described by the evolution of the interface
\partial D(t)

only.
The simplest example of a finite energy
vortex patch is the circular “Rankine" vortex
which is the circular steady solution with
D(t)=D(0)=\{|\zeta|\leq 1\}
at any time
t
.
On the other hand,
since for
\alpha\in\left(0,2\right)
there is no analogue of Yudovich theorem,
also to establish the local existence theory for sharp fronts nearby is a difficult task.
In the last few
years special
global in time sharp-front
solutions of
\alpha
-SQG
close to the Rankine vortex have been constructed:
the uniformly rotating
V
-states in

[
43
,
16
,
17
,
40
]
,
as well as time quasi-periodic solutions in
[
42
]
for

\alpha\in\left(0,\tfrac{1}{2}\right)
, and in
[
41
]
for
\alpha\in(1,2)
.
We quote further
literature after the statement of Theorem
1.1
.

In this work we prove the first long-time existence result of sharp fronts
of
\alpha
-SQG, in the more singular cases
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
for any initial interface
\partial D(0)
sufficiently smooth and close to a circular Rankine vortex, see
Theorem
1.1
. This is achieved thanks to the paralinearization
result of the
\alpha
-SQG sharp front equation in Theorem
4.1

for any
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
, that
we consider of independent interest in itself.

Let us present precisely our main results.
The evolution of the boundary of the vortex patch
is governed by the
Contour Dynamics Equation

for a parametrization

X:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
x\mapsto X(t,x)
, with
\mathbb{T}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{R}/\penalty 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
,
of the boundary
\partial D(t)
of the vortex patch.
The Contour Dynamics Equation for the
\alpha
-SQG patch
–also called sharp-fronts equation- is

\partial_{t}X\left(t,x\right)=\frac{c_{\alpha}}{2\pi}\ \int\frac{X^{\prime}\left(t,x\right)-X^{\prime}\left(t,y\right)}{\left|X\left(t,x\right)-X\left(t,y\right)\right|^{\alpha}}\,\textnormal{d}y\,,\quad\alpha\in\left(0,2\right)\,,

(1.4)

where
′
denotes the derivative with respect to
x
,

c_{\alpha}\vcentcolon=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}{2^{1-\alpha}\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}

(1.5)

and
\Gamma(\cdot)
is the Euler-Gamma function.
The local solvability of
Equation

1.4
in Sobolev class
has been proved
in
[
36
]
for
\alpha\in\left(0,1\right]
, if the
initial datum belongs to
H^{s}
,
s\geq 3

and in
[
37
,
38
]
for
less regular initial data (see
[
71
]

for
C^{\infty}
data). The uniqueness has been established in
[
25
]
.
For
\alpha\in\left(1,2\right)
the local existence and uniqueness theory
has been proved in
[
21
,
38
]
for initial data in
H^{s}
,
s\geq 4
, see also
[
62
,
1
]
.
In the very recent work
[
59
]
it is proved that the
\alpha
-patch problem
is ill posed in
W^{k,p}
if
p\neq 2
.

Very little is known concerning long time existence results.
Actually highly unstable dynamical behaviour could emerge.
In this context we mention the remarkable work
[
61
]

where two smooth patches of
opposite sign
develop
a finite time particle collision. We also quote
the numerical study
[
73
]
which provides
some evidence of the development of filaments, pointing to a possible formation of singularities via a self-similar filament cascade.

In this paper we consider sharp fronts
of
\alpha
-SQG that are a radial perturbation of the unitary circle, i.e.

X\left(x\right)=\left(1+h\left(x\right)\right)\vec{\gamma}\left(x\right)\,,\qquad\quad\vec{\gamma}(x)\vcentcolon=(\cos(x),\sin(x))\,.

(1.6)

Since
only the normal component
of the velocity field deforms the patch, one derives
from (
1.4
) a scalar evolution equation for
h(x)
.
Multiplying (
1.4
) by the
normal vector

n(x)=h^{\prime}(x)\vec{\gamma}^{\prime}(x)-\left(1+h(x)\right)\vec{\gamma}(x)
to the boundary
of the patch at
X(x)
,
we deduce that
h(t,x)
solves the equation

\displaystyle-\left(1+h\left(x\right)\right)\partial_{t}h\left(x\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{c_{\alpha}}{2\pi}\ \int\frac{\cos\left(x-y\right)\left[\left(1+h\left(x\right)\right)h^{\prime}\left(y\right)-\left(1+h\left(y\right)\right)h^{\prime}\left(x\right)\right]}{\left[\left(1+h\left(x\right)\right)^{2}+\left(1+h\left(y\right)\right)^{2}-2\left(1+h\left(x\right)\right)\left(1+h\left(y\right)\right)\cos\left(x-y\right)\right]^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\textnormal{d}y

(1.7)

\displaystyle+\frac{c_{\alpha}}{2\pi}\ \int\frac{\sin\left(x-y\right)\left[\left(1+h\left(x\right)\right)\left(1+h\left(y\right)\right)+h^{\prime}\left(x\right)h^{\prime}\left(y\right)\right]}{\left[\left(1+h\left(x\right)\right)^{2}+\left(1+h\left(y\right)\right)^{2}-2\left(1+h\left(x\right)\right)\left(1+h\left(y\right)\right)\cos\left(x-y\right)\right]^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\textnormal{d}y\,.

In view of
[
21
,
38
]

if
h_{0}\in H^{s}
,
for any

s\geq 4
,
there exists a unique solution
h\in\mathcal{C}\left(\left[0,T\right];H^{s}\right)
of (
1.7
)
defined up to a time

T>\frac{1}{C_{s}\ \left\|h_{0}\right\|_{H^{s}}}
.
The following result extends the local-existence result for longer times.

Theorem 1.1

(Quadratic life-span)
.

Let
\alpha\in\left(1,2\right)
.
There exists
s_{0}>0
such that for any
s\geq s_{0}
,
there are
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
c_{s,\alpha}>0
,

C_{s,\alpha}>0
such that, for any

h_{0}
in
H^{s}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}\right)
satisfying
\left\|h_{0}\right\|_{H^{s}}\leq\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
,
the
equation (
1.7
) with initial condition
h(0)=h_{0}

has a unique classical solution

h\in\mathcal{C}\left(\left[-T_{s,\alpha},T_{s,\alpha}\right];H^{s}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}\right)\right)\qquad\text{with}\qquad T_{s,\alpha}>c_{s,\alpha}\varepsilon^{-2}\,,

(1.8)

satisfying

\left\|h\left(t\right)\right\|_{H^{s}}\leq C_{s,\alpha}\,\varepsilon
, for any

t\in\left[-T_{s,\alpha},T_{s,\alpha}\right]
.

Theorem

1.1
is proved by normal form arguments for quasi-linear
Hamiltonian PDEs. The first important step is the
paralinearization
of
(
1.7
) once it has been written in Hamiltonian form,
see
Theorem

4.1
. The
paralinearization formula (
4.1
)
of the
\alpha
-SQG equations
holds for any
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
. It is a major result of
this paper, that we expect to be used also in other contexts.

In order to prove
Theorem

1.1

we reduce the paralinearized equation (
4.1
), for any
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
to Birkhoff normal form up to cubic smoothing
terms.
This requires to
prove the absence of
three wave interactions
, which is verified
in Lemma
3.5

by showing the convexity of the
linear normal frequencies of
the
\alpha
-SQG equation at the circular vortex patch.

Theorem
1.1
is the first Birkhoff normal form results for sharp fronts
equations.

In recent years several
advances have been obtained concerning long time existence of solutions for
quasi-linear equations in fluids dynamics on
\mathbb{T}
, namely
with periodic boundary conditions, as the water waves equations.
Quadratic life span of small amplitude solutions have been obtained in

[
6
,
55
,
52
,
51
,
76
,
56
,
57
,
2
]
,
extended to longer times in

[
5
,
7
,
6
,
10
,
34
,
75
,
78
,
12
]
,
by either introducing quasi-linear modified energies or using
Birkhoff normal form techniques.
We also quote the long time existence result

[
20
]
for solutions of SQG close to the infinite energy
radial solution
\left|\zeta\right|
.

Before explaining
in detail
the main ideas of proof we present further results in literature about

\alpha
-SQG.

Further literature.

Special infinite energy
global-in-time sharp front solutions have been constructed
in
[
29
,
53
,
54
]

if the initial patch is a small perturbation of the half-space,
by exploiting dispersive techniques. In
[
18
,
19
]
special global smooth solutions in the cases
\alpha=0,1
are obtained using bifurcation theory.
Concerning the possible formation of
singularities, we mention that
[
61
,
62
]

constructed
special initial sharp fronts in the half-space
which develop
a splash singularity in finite time if
\alpha\in\left(0,\frac{1}{12}\right)
,
later extended
in
[
38
]
for
\alpha\in\left(0,\frac{1}{3}\right)
.
We also mention that

[
39
,
60
]
have proved that, if
\alpha\in(0,1]
,
the sharp fronts equation in the whole space
does not generate finite-time singularities of splash type.

V-states.

The existence of uniformly rotating

V
-states close to the disk was first numerically investigated in
[
33
]
and analytically proved in
[
15
]
for the Euler equations,
recently extended to global branches in

[
44
]
.
For
\alpha
-SQG equations, as already mentioned,
local bifurcation results
of sharp fronts from the disk have been obtained in

[
43
,
16
,
17
,
18
,
40
,
19
]
.
Smooth
V
-states
bifurcating from different steady configurations have been constructed
for
\alpha\in[0,2)
in

[
31
,
69
,
48
,
47
,
46
,
32
,
30
,
50
,
49
]
. We refer to the introductions in

[
42
,
41
]
for more references.

Quasi-periodic solutions.

As already mentioned
global in time quasi-periodic solutions of the
\alpha
-SQG
vortex patch equation
close to the circle (
1.6
)
have been recently constructed in
[
42
]
for

\alpha\in\left(0,\tfrac{1}{2}\right)

and
in
[
41
]
for
\alpha\in(1,2)
.
The result
[
42
]
holds for
“most" values of
\alpha\in\left(0,\tfrac{1}{2}\right)
(used a parameter to impose non-resonance conditions)
whereas
[
41
]
holds for any
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
using the initial conditions as parameters,
via a Birkhoff normal form analysis.
The
2D
-Euler equation is more degenerate
and in this case quasi-periodic solutions have been constructed
in
[
9
]
close to the family of
Kirkhoff ellipses, not only close to the disk
(we refer to
[
9
]
for a wider introduction to the field and literature
about quasi-periodic solutions).
These results build on on KAM techniques
[
3
,
4
,
8
,
13
,
35
]
developed for the water waves equations.

Ideas of the proof

The average-preserving unknown and the Hamiltonian formulation.

The equation
(
1.7
) for the
unknown
h(x)
is
not
convenient
because its evolution does not preserve the average and
it is
not
Hamiltonian.
This problem is overcome
by reformulating (
1.7
)
in terms of the variable

f\left(x\right)\vcentcolon=h\left(x\right)+\tfrac{1}{2}h^{2}\left(x\right)\,.

(1.9)

Indeed, symmetrizing in the
x,y
variables, we get

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\,\text{r.h.s. \eqref{eq:elev_funct_eq}}\,\textnormal{d}y=0

and then

\frac{\textnormal{d}}{\textnormal{d}t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left(h\left(t,x\right)+\tfrac{1}{2}h^{2}\left(t,x\right)\right)\textnormal{d}x=0
.
Thus the average of

f\left(x\right)
in (
1.9
)
is preserved along the patch evolution.
Note that, inverting (
1.9
) for small
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}
and
\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}
,
we have

h\left(x\right)=\sqrt{1+2f\left(x\right)}-1

and the Sobolev norms of
f\left(x\right)
and
h\left(x\right)
are equivalent

\left\|f\right\|_{s}\sim\left\|h\right\|_{s}\,,\qquad\forall s>\tfrac{1}{2}\,.

(1.10)

Remark 1.2
.

There is a deep connection between the conservation of the average of
f(x)
and the incompressibility of the flow generated by the
\alpha
-SQG patch.
Actually the Lebesgue measure
\textnormal{Vol}\left(t\right)
of the finite region of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
enclosed by the patch is, passing to polar coordinates,

\textnormal{Vol}\left(t\right)=\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\int_{0}^{1+h\left(t,x\right)}\rho\ \textnormal{d}\rho\ \textnormal{d}x=\pi+\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\left(h\left(t,x\right)+\frac{h^{2}\left(t,x\right)}{2}\right)\textnormal{d}x=\pi+\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}f\left(t,x\right)\textnormal{d}x

and therefore the conservation of the average
\int_{\mathbb{T}}f\left(x\right)\ \textnormal{d}x

amounts to the conservation of

\textnormal{Vol}\left(t\right)
.

The variable (
1.9
)
has been used in
[
42
]
where it is also
proved that the evolution equation for
f
has a Hamiltonian structure,
see also
[
41
]
.
The following result is
[
42
, Proposition 2.1]
:

Proposition 1.3

(Hamiltonian formulation of (
1.7
))
.

Let
\alpha\in\left(0,2\right)
.
If
h
is a solution of
Eq.

1.7
then the variable
f
defined in (
1.9
) solves the Hamiltonian equation

\partial_{t}f=\partial_{x}\,\nabla E_{\alpha}\left(f\right)

(1.11)

where
E_{\alpha}\left(f\right)
is the pseudo-energy of the patch
whose
L^{2}
-gradient
\nabla E_{\alpha}\left(f\right)
is

\nabla E_{\alpha}\left(f\right)\\
=\frac{c_{\alpha}}{2\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\mathop{\,\hbox to0.0pt{--\hss}\!\!\!\int}\nolimits\frac{1+2f\left(y\right)+\sqrt{1+2f\left(x\right)}\ \partial_{y}\left[\sqrt{1+2f\left(y\right)}\sin\left(x-y\right)\right]}{\left[1+2f\left(x\right)+1+2f\left(y\right)-2\sqrt{1+2f\left(x\right)}\sqrt{1+2f\left(y\right)}\cos\left(x-y\right)\right]^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\ \textnormal{d}y\,.

(1.12)

Note that the evolution equation (
1.11
) is
translation-invariant because

E_{\alpha}\circ\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}=E_{\alpha}
for any

\varsigma\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}f(x):=f(x+\varsigma)
. Moreover, in view of the presence of the Poisson tensor
\partial_{x}
in
(
1.11
) it is evident that the space average
of
f
is a prime integral of (
1.11
). In the sequel we
assume the space average of
f
to be zero.

Paralinearization of (
1.11
) for
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
.

Section

4
is dedicated to write the Hamiltonian equation (
1.11
) in paradifferential form and to provide the detailed
structure of the principal and subprincipal symbols in the expansion of the paradifferential operator, obtaining

\partial_{t}f+\partial_{x}\circ\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[\left(1+\nu\left(f;x\right)\right)L_{\alpha}\left(\left|\xi\right|\right)+V\left(f;x\right)+P\left(f;x,\xi\right)\right]f=\textnormal{smoothing terms}

(1.13)

where (see
Theorem

4.1
for a detailed statement)

•

\left(1+\nu\left(f;x\right)\right)L_{\alpha}\left(\left|\xi\right|\right)+V\left(f;x\right)

is a real symbol of order
\max\{\alpha-1,0\}
and
\nu\left(f;x\right)
,

V\left(f;x\right)
are real functions vanishing for
f=0
;

•

the symbol
P\left(f;x,\xi\right)
has order
-1
and vanishes for
f=0
.

We note that in (
1.13
) the operator
\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[\ \right]

is the paradifferential quantization according to Weyl
(see Definition
2.10
)
and thus

\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[{1+\nu\left(f;x\right)}L_{\alpha}\left(\left|\xi\right|\right)+V\left(f;x\right)\right]

is self-adjoint. As a consequence the linear Hamiltonian operator

\partial_{x}\circ\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[{1+\nu\left(f;x\right)}L_{\alpha}\left(\left|\xi\right|\right)+V\left(f;x\right)\right]

is skew-self-adjoint at positive orders. This is the reason why the unbounded quasi-linear
vector field

\partial_{x}\circ\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[{1+\nu\left(f;x\right)}L_{\alpha}\left(\left|\xi\right|\right)+V\left(f;x\right)\right]f

admits energy estimates in Sobolev spaces
H^{s}
via commutator estimates
(actually existence and unicity of the
solutions of
(
1.13
) would follow as in
[
11
]
).
We remark the absence in (
1.13
) of operators like

\partial_{x}\circ\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[\text{symbol of order }(\alpha-2)\right]
.
The cancellations of such terms are verified in
Appendix

A

by a direct calculus
and it is ultimately a consequence of the Hamiltonian structure of the equation
(
1.11
).

We also note that
the equation (
1.13
) can be written, in homogeneity degrees, as

\partial_{t}f+\omega_{\alpha}(D)f=\mathcal{O}\left(f^{2}\right)\,\qquad\text{where}\qquad\omega_{\alpha}(D):=\partial_{x}\circ L_{\alpha}\left(\left|D\right|\right)

(1.14)

is the unperturbed dispersion relation.

Let us
explain how we deduce the paralinearization formula
(
1.13
) in Section

4
.
The nonlinear
term
\nabla E_{\alpha}\left(f\right)
in (
1.11
) can be written as a
convolution operator

\nabla E_{\alpha}\left(f\right)\left(x\right)=\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}K\left(f;x,z\right)\frac{f\left(x\right)-f\left(x-z\right)}{\left|z\right|^{\alpha}}\,\textnormal{d}z

with a nonlinear real valued convolution kernel
K\left(f;x,z\right)
.
By Taylor expanding the kernel
z\mapsto K\left(f;x,z\right)
at
z=0

(provided
f
is sufficiently regular) and expanding in paraproducts the arguments of the above integral, we obtain an expansion of the form

\displaystyle\nabla E_{\alpha}\left(f\right)\left(x\right)=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{\mathsf{j}=0}^{\mathsf{J}}\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[K_{\mathsf{j}}\left(f,\ldots,f^{\left(\mathsf{j+1}\right)};x\right)\right]\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\left(f\left(x\right)-f\left(x-z\right)\right)\ \frac{z^{\mathsf{j}}}{\left|z\right|^{\alpha}}\ \textnormal{d}z

(1.15a)

\displaystyle+\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[R\left(f,\ldots,f^{\left(\mathsf{j+1}\right)};x,z\right)\right]\left(f\left(x\right)-f\left(x-z\right)\right)\textnormal{d}z+\textnormal{ smoothing terms },

(1.15b)

where
R\left(f,\ldots,f^{\left(\mathsf{j+1}\right)};x,z\right)=\mathpzc{o}\left(\left|z\right|^{\mathsf{J}-\alpha}\right)
as
z\to 0

being the Taylor remainder at order
\mathsf{J}

(here
f^{\left(\mathsf{j}\right)}(x)
denotes the
\mathsf{j}
-derivative of

f(x)
).
The terms in the finite sum (
1.15a
)
are particularly simple paradifferential operators. Indeed, provided
\alpha<2
,

\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\left(f\left(x\right)-f\left(x-z\right)\right)\ \frac{z^{\mathsf{j}}}{\left|z\right|^{\alpha}}\ \textnormal{d}z=\mathbb{V}_{\alpha-\mathsf{j}}f+m_{\alpha-\left(\mathsf{j}+1\right)}\left(D\right)f

where
\mathbb{V}_{\alpha-\mathsf{j}}
is a real constant
and
m_{\alpha-\left(\mathsf{j}+1\right)}\left(\xi\right)
is a
Fourier multiplier of order
\alpha-\left(\mathsf{j}+1\right)
, as follows
by standard asymptotics of singular integral operators, see
[
74
]
.
Thus, by symbolic calculus,

\small\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[K_{\mathsf{j}}\right]\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\left(f\left(x\right)-f\left(x-z\right)\right)\ \frac{z^{\mathsf{j}}}{\left|z\right|^{\alpha}}\ \textnormal{d}z=\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[V_{\alpha-\mathsf{j}}\left(f,\ldots,f^{\left(\mathsf{j+1}\right)};x\right)+K_{\mathsf{j}}\left(f,\ldots,f^{\left(\mathsf{j+1}\right)};x\right)m_{\alpha-\left(\mathsf{j}+1\right)}\left(\xi\right)\right]f+\textnormal{l.o.t.},

where
V_{\alpha-\mathsf{j}}
are real functions.
The unbounded terms

\partial_{x}\circ\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[K_{\mathsf{j}}\left(f,\ldots,f^{\left(\mathsf{j+1}\right)};x\right)m_{\alpha-\left(\mathsf{j}+1\right)}\left(\xi\right)\right]f
,
\mathsf{j}=0,1
,
would induces a loss of derivatives in the
H^{s}
energy estimates
if the imaginary part

\textnormal{Im}\,m_{\alpha-\left(\mathsf{j}+1\right)}\left(\xi\right)\neq 0
.
Therefore a detailed analysis of these symbols is essential.
The highest order Fourier multiplier
m_{\alpha-1}(\xi)

turns out to be real. Concerning the subprincipal symbol

K_{1}\left(f,f^{\prime};x\right)m_{\alpha-2}(\xi)
, it turns out that

m_{\alpha-2}(\xi)
has a non-zero imaginary part but
a subtle
nonlinear cancellation
reveals that the corresponding
coefficient
K_{1}\left(f,f^{\prime};x\right)

is identically zero, as verified in
Appendix

A
.
Such a structure, which ultimately stems by the Hamiltonian nature of (
1.11
),
could be proven up to an arbitrary negative order.

Concerning the first term in (
1.15b
), we use
that
R\left(f,\ldots,f^{\left(\mathsf{j+1}\right)};x,z\right)
is
\mathpzc{o}\left(\left|z\right|^{\mathsf{J}-\alpha}\right)
as
z\to 0
so that, modulo regularizing operators, it can be expressed as a paradifferential operator of order
\alpha-(\mathsf{J}+1)
, which is a bounded vector field
taking
\mathsf{J}\geq 2
, see
2.36
.

Reduction of (
1.13
) to Birkhoff normal form up to cubic terms.

In Section
5
we first conjugate the paradifferential equation
(
1.13
)
into an equation with constant coefficient symbols, modulo smoothing operators,

\partial_{t}\mathpzc{g}+\partial_{x}\circ\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[\left(1+\mathpzc{c}_{0}\left(f\right)\right)L_{\alpha}\left(\left|\xi\right|\right)+\mathsf{H}_{\alpha}\left(f;\xi\right)\right]\mathpzc{g}=\text{smoothing terms}

(1.16)

where
\mathpzc{c}_{0}\left(f\right)
is the average of a real nonlinear function of

\nu{\left(f;x\right)}

and
\mathsf{H}_{\alpha}\left(f;\xi\right)
is a
x
-independent symbol with imaginary part

\textnormal{Im}\mathsf{H}_{\alpha}\left(f;\xi\right)
of order
-1
, see
Proposition
5.2
. Thus
(
1.16
) is still Hamiltonian up to order zero and
thus it satisfies
H^{s}
-energy estimates.
The unknowns
\mathpzc{g}\left(t\right)
and
f\left(t\right)
have equivalent Sobolev norms

\left\|\mathpzc{g}\left(t\right)\right\|_{s}\sim_{s,\alpha}\left\|f\left(t\right)\right\|_{s}
.
We remark that in (
1.16
)
the constant
\mathpzc{c}_{0}\left(f\right)
and the symbol

\mathsf{H}_{\alpha}\left(f;\xi\right)
vanish
quadratically at
f=0
and thus
the only term which can disturb the quadratic life span
of the solution
\mathpzc{g}\left(t\right)

is the smoothing operator
R_{1}\left(f\right)
in the decomposition

\text{smoothing terms}=R_{1}\left(f\right)\mathpzc{g}+R_{\geq 2}\left(f\right)\mathpzc{g}\,.

Then in Lemma
5.7
we implement a Birkhoff
normal form step to cancel
R_{1}\left(f\right)\mathpzc{g}
.
An algebraic ingredient is to verify the absence of three wave interactions, namely that,
for any
n,j,k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
satisfying
k=j+n
,

\left|\omega_{\alpha}\left(k\right)-\omega_{\alpha}\left(j\right)-\omega_{\alpha}\left(n\right)\right|\geq c>0\,,

where
\omega_{\alpha}\left(j\right)
are the normal
\alpha
-SQG frequencies in
(
1.14
). Such a property follows by proving the
convexity
of the
the map
\omega_{\alpha}\left(j\right)
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
see
Lemma

3.5
.

The final outcome is an
energy estimate
for any
small enough
solution of (
1.11
) of the form

\left\|f\left(t\right)\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}}\lesssim_{s,\alpha}\left\|f\left(0\right)\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|f\left(\tau\right)\right\|^{4}_{H^{s}}\,\textnormal{d}\tau\,,\qquad t>0\,,

which implies Theorem
1.1
.

Structure of the manuscript.

Section
2
contains the paradifferential calculus used along the paper.
In Section
2.1

we report the main results in
[
5
,
10
]
. Then
in Section
2.2
we introduce a
z
-dependent
paradifferential calculus used for the paralinearization
of (
1.13
)
in
Section

4
.

Section

3
is dedicated to the linearization of (
1.11
) at the stationary state
f\equiv 0
.

Lemmas

3.1
and
3.6
extend
to any
\alpha\in\left(0,2\right)
the asymptotic expansions of the
normal frequencies
\omega_{\alpha}\left(j\right)

proved in
[
42
]
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
In
Section

4
we provide
the paralinearization (
1.13
) of the Hamiltonian equation
(
1.11
) for any
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
.
In Section
5
we conjugate the paradifferential equation
(
1.13
) into an equation with constant coefficients, modulo smoothing operators.
In
Section

5
we perform the
Birkhoff normal form step and prove Theorem
1.1
.

Notation.

We denote with
C
a positive constant which does not depend on any parameter of the problem. We write
A\lesssim_{c_{1},\ldots,c_{M}}B
if
A\leq C\left(c_{1},\ldots,c_{M}\right)B
and
A\sim_{c_{1},\ldots,c_{M}}B
if
A\lesssim_{c_{1},\ldots,c_{M}}B
and
B\lesssim_{c_{1},\ldots,c_{M}}A
.
We denote with
\mathbb{N}=1,2,\ldots
the set of natural numbers and

\mathbb{N}_{0}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{N}\cup\left\{0\right\}
. For any
x\geq 0
we denote

\left\lceil x\right\rceil\vcentcolon=\min\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\ \middle|\ x\leq n\right\}
.
We denote
\mathbb{T}\vcentcolon=\mathbb{R}\setminus(2\pi\mathbb{Z})
the one-dimensional torus
with norm
\left|x\right|_{\mathbb{T}}\vcentcolon=\inf_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|x+2\pi j\right|
.
We denote
D=-{\rm i}\partial_{x}
and
\left\llbracket A\,,\,B\right\rrbracket
the commutator
\left\llbracket A\,,\,B\right\rrbracket=AB-BA=:\textnormal{Ad}_{A}B
.
Given a linear real self-adjoint operator
A
any operator of the form
\partial_{x}\circ A
will be referred as
linear Hamiltonian
.
We denote
\mathop{\,\hbox to0.0pt{--\hss}\!\!\!\int}\nolimits\bullet\textnormal{d}x=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\bullet\textnormal{d}x
.

2
Functional setting

Along the paper we deal with real parameters

s\geq s_{0}\gg K\gg\rho\gg N\geq 0

(2.1)

where
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
The values of
s,s_{0},K
and
\rho
may vary from line to line while still being true the relation (
2.1
).
For the proof of Theorem
1.1
we shall take
N=1
.

We expand a
2\pi
-periodic function
u(x)
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C})
in Fourier series as

u(x)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{u}\left(j\right)e^{{\rm i}jx}\,,\qquad\hat{u}\left(j\right)\vcentcolon={\cal F}_{x\to j}\left(j\right)\vcentcolon=u_{j}\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)e^{-{\rm i}jx}\,{\rm d}x\,.

(2.2)

A function
u(x)
is real if and only if
\overline{u_{j}}=u_{-j}
, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}
.
For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
we define the Sobolev space
H^{s}\vcentcolon=H^{s}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C})

with norm

\left\|u\right\|_{s}\vcentcolon=\left\|u\right\|_{H^{s}}=\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\langle j\right\rangle^{2s}\left|\hat{u}\left(j\right)\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,,\qquad\left\langle j\right\rangle\vcentcolon=\max(1,|j|)\,.

We define
\Pi_{0}u\vcentcolon=\hat{u}_{0}
the average of
u
and

\Pi_{0}^{\bot}\vcentcolon=\textnormal{Id}-\Pi_{0}\,.

(2.3)

We define
H^{s}_{0}
the subspace of zero average functions of
H^{s}
, for which
we also denote
\left\|u\right\|_{s}=\left\|u\right\|_{H^{s}}=\left\|u\right\|_{H^{s}_{0}}
.
Clearly
H^{0}_{0}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C})=L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C})

with scalar product, for any
u,v\in L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C})
,

\langle u,v\rangle_{L^{2}_{0}}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)\,\overline{{v(x)}}\,{\rm d}x\,.

(2.4)

Given an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
symmetric with respect to
t=0

and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the space

C_{*}^{K}\left(I;H_{0}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{X}\right)\right)\vcentcolon=\bigcap_{k=0}^{K}C^{k}\left(I;H_{0}^{s-\alpha k}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{X}\right)\right)\,,\qquad\quad\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}\,,\ \mathbb{C}\,,

resp.
C^{K}_{*}(I;H^{s}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{X}))
,
endowed with the norm

\sup_{t\in I}\left\|u(t,\cdot)\right\|_{K,s}\qquad{\rm where}\qquad\left\|u(t,\cdot)\right\|_{K,s}\vcentcolon=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\left\|\partial_{t}^{k}u(t,\cdot)\right\|_{H^{s-\alpha k}}\,.

(2.5)

We denote
B^{K}_{s}(I;\epsilon_{0})
, resp.
B^{K}_{s,\mathbb{R}}(I;\epsilon_{0})
,
the ball of radius
\epsilon_{0}>0
in
C_{*}^{K}(I,H_{0}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C})\right)
,
resp. in
C_{*}^{K}(I,H_{0}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R})\right)
. We
also we define
B_{C^{K}_{*}(I,H^{s}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}))}\left(0;\epsilon_{0}\right)
the ball of center zero and radius
\epsilon_{0}
in
C^{K}_{*}(I,H^{s}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}))
.

Remark 2.1
.

The parameter
s
in (
2.5
) denotes the spatial Sobolev regularity of the solution
u(t,\cdot)

and
K
its regularity in the time variable.
The
\alpha
-SQG vector field
loses
\alpha
-derivatives,
and therefore, differentiating the solution
u(t)
for
k
-times in the time variable,
there is a loss of
\alpha k
-spatial derivatives.
The parameter
\rho
in (
2.1
) denotes the order where we decide to stop our regularization of the system.

We set some further notation.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
we denote by
\Pi_{n}
the orthogonal projector from

L^{2}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C})
to the linear subspace spanned by

\{e^{{\rm i}nx},e^{-{\rm i}nx}\}
,

(\Pi_{n}u)(x)\vcentcolon=\hat{u}(n)e^{{\rm i}nx}+\hat{u}(-n)e^{-{\rm i}nx}
.
If
\,\mathcal{U}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{p})
is a
p
-tuple of functions and
\vec{n}=(n_{1},\dots,n_{p})\in\mathbb{N}^{p}
, we set

\Pi_{\vec{n}}\mathcal{U}\vcentcolon=\big(\Pi_{n_{1}}u_{1},\dots,\Pi_{n_{p}}u_{p}\big)
and

\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}\mathcal{U}\vcentcolon=\big(\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}u_{1},\dots,\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}u_{p}\big)
,
where
\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}
is the translation operator

\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}\colon u(x)\mapsto u(x+\varsigma)\,.

(2.6)

For
\vec{\jmath}_{p}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{p})\in\mathbb{Z}^{p}

we denote

|\vec{\jmath}_{p}|\vcentcolon=\max(|j_{1}|,\ldots,|j_{p}|)
and

u_{\vec{\jmath}_{p}}\vcentcolon=u_{j_{1}}\dots u_{j_{p}}
.
Note that
the Fourier coefficients of
\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}u
are

(\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}u)_{j}=e^{{\rm i}j\varsigma}u_{j}
.

A vector field
X(u)
is
translation invariant
if

X\circ\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}=\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}\circ X

for any
\varsigma\in\mathbb{R}
.

Given a linear operator
R(u)[\cdot]
acting on
L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C})

we associate the linear operator defined by the relation

\overline{R(u)}v\vcentcolon=\overline{R(u)\overline{v}}
for any
v\in L^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C})

An operator
R(u)
is
real
if
R(u)=\overline{R(u)}
for any
u
real.

2.1
Paradifferential calculus

We introduce paradifferential operators (Definition
2.10
)
following
[
5
]
, with minor modifications
due to the fact that
we deal with a scalar equation and not a system, and the fact that
we consider operators acting on
H_{0}^{s}
and
H^{s}
and not
on homogenous spaces
\dot{H}^{s}
. In this way we will mainly rely on
results in
[
5
,
10
]
.

Classes of symbols.

Roughly speaking the class
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{p}^{m}
contains symbols of order
m
and homogeneity
p
in
u
, whereas the class
\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}
contains non-homogeneous symbols of order
m
that vanish at degree at least
p
in
u
and that are
(K-K^{\prime})
-times differentiable in
t
. We can think the parameter
K^{\prime}
like the number of time derivatives of
u
that are contained in the symbols.
We denote
H_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C})\vcentcolon=\bigcap_{s\in\mathbb{R}}H_{0}^{s}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C})
.

Definition 2.2

(Symbols)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
,
p,N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
K^{\prime}\leq K
, and
\epsilon_{0}>0
.

i)

p
-
homogeneous symbols.
We denote by
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}
the space of symmetric
p
-linear maps from
\left(H_{0}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}\right)\right)^{p}
to the space of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
functions from
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{C}
,

(x,\xi)\mapsto a(\mathcal{U};x,\xi)
, satisfying the following: there exist
\mu\geq 0
and, for any
\gamma,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
there is a constant
C>0
such that

\left|\partial_{x}^{\gamma}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}a\left(\Pi_{\vec{n}}\mathcal{U};x,\xi\right)\right|\leq C|\vec{n}|^{\mu+\gamma}\langle\xi\rangle^{m-\beta}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left\|\Pi_{n_{j}}u_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}}

(2.7)

for any
\mathcal{U}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{p})\in\left(H_{0}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}\right)\right)^{p}
and
\vec{n}=(n_{1},\dots,n_{p})\in\mathbb{N}^{p}
.
Moreover we assume that, if for some
(n_{0},\dots,n_{p})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\times\mathbb{N}^{p}
,
\Pi_{n_{0}}a\left(\Pi_{\vec{n}}\mathcal{U};\cdot\right)\not=0
, then there exists a choice of signs
\eta_{j}\in\{\pm 1\}
such that
\sum_{j=1}^{p}\eta_{j}n_{j}=n_{0}
. In addition we require the translation invariance property

a\left(\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}\mathcal{U};x,\xi\right)=a\left(\mathcal{U};x+\varsigma,\xi\right),\quad\forall\varsigma\in\mathbb{R}\,,

(2.8)

where
\mathsf{t}_{\varsigma}
is the translation operator in (
2.6
).

For
p=0
we denote by
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{0}
the space of constant coefficients symbols
\xi\mapsto a(\xi)
which satisfy (
2.7
) with
\gamma=0
and the right hand side replaced by
C\langle\xi\rangle^{m-\beta}
and we call them Fourier multipliers.

ii)

Non-homogeneous symbols.
We denote by
\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}[\epsilon_{0}]
the space of functions
a(u;t,x,\xi)
,
defined for
u\in B_{s_{0}}^{K^{\prime}}(I;\epsilon_{0})
for some
s_{0}
large enough, with complex values, such that for any
0\leq k\leq K-K^{\prime}
, any
s\geq s_{0}
, there are
C>0
,
0<\epsilon_{0}(s)<\epsilon_{0}
and for any
u\in B_{s_{0}}^{K}\left(I;\epsilon_{0}(s)\right)\cap C_{*}^{k+K^{\prime}}\left(I,H_{0}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}\right)\right)
and any
\gamma,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, with
\gamma\leq s-s_{0}
one has the estimate

\left|\partial_{t}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\gamma}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}a\left(u;t,x,\xi\right)\right|\leq C\langle\xi\rangle^{m-\beta}\|u\|_{k+K^{\prime},s_{0}}^{p-1}\|u\|_{k+K^{\prime},s}\,.

(2.9)

If
p=0
the right hand side has to be replaced by
C\langle\xi\rangle^{m-\beta}
. We say that a non-homogeneous symbol
a(u;x,\xi)
is
real
if it is real valued for any

u\in B^{K^{\prime}}_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}(I;\epsilon_{0})
.

iii)

Symbols.
We denote by
\Sigma\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}[\epsilon_{0},N]
the space of
symbols

a(u;t,x,\xi)=\sum_{q=p}^{N}a_{q}\left(u,\ldots,u;x,\xi\right)+a_{>N}(u;t,x,\xi)

where
a_{q}
,
q=p,\dots,N

are homogeneous symbols in
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{q}^{m}
and

a_{>N}
is
a non-homogeneous symbol in
\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}^{m}
.

We say that a symbol
a(u;t,x,\xi)
is
real
if it is real valued for any

u\in B^{K^{\prime}}_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}(I;\epsilon_{0})
.

Notation 2.3
.

If
a(\mathcal{U};\cdot)
is a
p
-homogenous symbol we also denote
a(u)\vcentcolon=a(u,\ldots,u;\cdot)
the corresponding polynomial and we identify the
p
-homogeneous monomial
a(u;\cdot)
with the
p
-linear symmetric form
a(\mathcal{U};\cdot)
.

Actually also the non-homogeneous component of the symbols that we will
encounter in Section
4
depends
on time and space only through
u
, but since this information
is not needed it is not included in Definition
2.2
(as in
[
5
]
).

Remark 2.4
.

If
a(\mathcal{U};\cdot)
is a homogeneous
symbol in
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{p}^{m}
then

a(u,\ldots,u;\cdot)
belongs to
\Gamma^{m}_{K,0,p}[\epsilon_{0}]
, for any
\epsilon_{0}>0
.

Remark 2.5
.

If
a
is a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]

then
\partial_{x}a\in\Sigma\Gamma^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]
and

\partial_{\xi}a\in\Sigma\Gamma^{m-1}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]
.
If in addition
b
is a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{m^{\prime}}_{K,K^{\prime},p^{\prime}}[\epsilon_{0},N]
then

ab\in\Sigma\Gamma^{m+m^{\prime}}_{K,K^{\prime},p+p^{\prime}}[\epsilon_{0},N]
.

Remark 2.6

( Fourier representation of symbols)
.

The translation invariance property (
2.8
)
means that the dependence with respect to the variable
x
of a symbol

a(\mathcal{U};x,\xi)
enters only through the functions
\mathcal{U}(x)
,
implying that a symbol

a_{q}(u;x,\xi)
in
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{q}^{m}
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
, has the form

a_{q}(u;x,\xi)=\!\!\!\!\sum_{\vec{\jmath}_{q}\in{(\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}})^{q}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\left(a_{q}\right)_{\vec{\jmath}_{q}}(\xi)u_{j_{1}}\cdots u_{j_{q}}e^{{\rm i}\left(j_{1}+\cdots+j_{q}\right)x}

(2.10)

where
(a_{q})_{\vec{\jmath}}(\xi)\in\mathbb{C}
are Fourier multipliers of order
m
satisfying: there exists
\mu\geq 0
, and
for any
\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there is
C_{\beta}>0
such that

\left|\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\left(a_{q}\right)_{\vec{\jmath}_{q}}(\xi)\right|\leq C_{\beta}\left|\vec{\jmath}_{q}\right|^{\mu}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{m-\beta},\quad\forall\vec{\jmath}_{q}\in(\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\})^{q}\,.

(2.11)

A symbol

a_{q}(u;x,\xi)
as in (
2.10
) is real if

\overline{\left(a_{q}\right)_{\vec{\jmath}_{q}}(\xi)}=\left(a_{q}\right)_{-\vec{\jmath}_{q}}(\xi)

(2.12)

By (
2.10
)
a symbol

a_{1}
in
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}^{m}

can be written as

a_{1}(u;x,\xi)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\end{subarray}}(a_{1})_{j}(\xi)u_{j}e^{{\rm i}jx}
, and therefore,
if
a_{1}
is
independent
of
x
, it is actually
a_{1}\equiv 0
.

We also define classes of functions in analogy with our classes of symbols.

Definition 2.7

(Functions)
.

Let
p,N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
K^{\prime}\leq K
,
\epsilon_{0}>0
.
We denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
, resp.
\mathcal{F}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0}]
,

\Sigma\mathcal{F}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]
,
the subspace of
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{0}_{p}
, resp.
\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0}]
,
resp.
\Sigma\Gamma^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]
,
made of those symbols which are independent of
\xi
.
We write
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{p}
, resp.
\mathcal{F}_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{\mathbb{R}}[\epsilon_{0}]
,

\Sigma\mathcal{F}_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{\mathbb{R}}[\epsilon_{0},N]
, to denote functions in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{p}
,
resp.
\mathcal{F}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0}]
,
\Sigma\mathcal{F}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]
,
which are real valued for any
u\in B^{K^{\prime}}_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}(I;\epsilon_{0})
.

The above class of symbols is closed under composition by a change of variables,
see
[
5
, Lemma 3.23]
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
K^{\prime}\leq K\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
,
p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
p\leq N
,
\epsilon_{0}>0
small enough. Consider
a symbol
a
in

{{\Sigma\Gamma}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]}
and functions
b,c
in
{{\Sigma{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,K^{\prime},1}[\epsilon_{0},N]}}
. Then

a\bigl(v;t,x+b(v;t,x),\xi(1+c(v;t,x))\bigr)

is in
{{\Sigma\Gamma}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]}
. In particular, if

a
is a function
in
{{\Sigma{\mathcal{F}}}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]}
, then
a(v;t,x+b(v;t,x))
is in
{{\Sigma{\mathcal{F}}}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]}
.

The following result is
[
5
, Lemma 3.21]
.

Lemma 2.9

(Inverse diffeomorphism)
.

Let
0\leq K^{\prime}\leq K
be in
\mathbb{N}
and
\beta(f;t,x)
be a real function

\beta(f;t,\cdot)
in
\Sigma{\cal F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,K^{\prime},1}[\epsilon_{0},N]
.
If
s_{0}
is large enough, and
f\in B^{K}_{s_{0}}\left(I;\epsilon_{0}\right)
then the
map

\Phi_{f}:x\to x+\beta(f;t,x)

is, for
\epsilon_{0}
small enough, a diffeomorphism of
\,{{\mathbb{T}}^{1}}
, and
its inverse diffeomorphism may be written as

\Phi_{f}^{-1}:y\to y+\breve{\beta}(f;t,y)

for some
\breve{\beta}
in
{{\Sigma{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,K^{\prime},1}[\epsilon_{0},N]}}
.

Paradifferential quantization.

Given
p\in\mathbb{N}
we consider
admissible cut-off
functions

\psi_{p}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
and
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
,
even with respect to each of their arguments, satisfying, for
0<\delta\ll 1
,

\displaystyle{\rm{supp}}\,\psi_{p}\subset\left\{(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R};|\xi^{\prime}|\leq\delta\langle\xi\rangle\right\}\,,\qquad\psi_{p}(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\equiv 1\,\,\,\rm{for}\,\,\,|\xi^{\prime}|\leq\delta\langle\xi\rangle/2\,,

(2.13)

\displaystyle\rm{supp}\,\psi\subset\left\{(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R};|\xi^{\prime}|\leq\delta\langle\xi\rangle\right\}\,,\qquad\quad\psi(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\equiv 1\,\,\,\rm{for}\,\,\,|\xi^{\prime}|\leq\delta\langle\xi\rangle/2\,.

(2.14)

For
p=0
we set
\psi_{0}\equiv 1
.
We assume moreover that

\left|\partial_{\xi}^{\gamma}\partial_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\beta}\psi_{p}(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\right|\leq C_{\gamma,\beta}\langle\xi\rangle^{-\gamma-|\beta|}\,,\ \forall\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0},\,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{p}\,,\ \ \left|\partial_{\xi}^{\gamma}\partial_{\xi^{\prime}}^{\beta}\psi(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\right|\leq C_{\gamma,\beta}\langle\xi\rangle^{-\gamma-\beta},\ \forall\gamma,\,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\,.

(2.15)

If
a(x,\xi)
is a smooth symbol
we define its Weyl quantization as the operator
acting on a

2\pi
-periodic function

u(x)
(written as in (
2.2
))
as

{\rm Op}^{W}\left[a\right]u=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{a}\left(k-j,\frac{k+j}{2}\right)\hat{u}\left(j\right)\right)e^{{\rm i}kx}

(2.16)

where
\hat{a}(k,\xi)
is the
k
-Fourier coefficient of the
2\pi-
periodic function
x\mapsto a(x,\xi)
.

Definition 2.10

(Bony-Weyl quantization)
.

If
a
is a symbol in
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}
,
respectively in
\Gamma^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0}]
,
we set

\displaystyle a_{\psi_{p}}(\mathcal{U};x,\xi)\vcentcolon=\sum_{\vec{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}\psi_{p}\left(\vec{n},\xi\right)\ a\left(\Pi_{\vec{n}}\mathcal{U};x,\xi\right)\,,

\displaystyle a_{\psi}(u;t,x,\xi)\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(\xi^{\prime},\xi)\hat{a}\left(u;t,\xi^{\prime},\xi\right)e^{{\rm i}\xi^{\prime}x}{\rm d}\xi^{\prime}\,,

where
\hat{a}
stands for the Fourier transform with respect to the
x
variable, and
we define the
Bony-Weyl
quantization of
a
as

\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a(\mathcal{U};\cdot)\right]={\rm Op}^{W}\left[a_{\psi_{p}}\left(\mathcal{U};\cdot\right)\right]\,,\qquad\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a(u;t,\cdot)\right]={\rm Op}^{W}\left[a_{\psi}\left(u;t,\cdot\right)\right]\,.

(2.17)

If
a
is a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]
,
we define its
Bony-Weyl
quantization

\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a(u;t,\cdot)\right]=\sum_{q=p}^{N}\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a_{q}(u,\ldots,u;\cdot)\right]+\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a_{>N}(u;t,\cdot)\right]\,.

Remark 2.11
.

\bullet

The operator

\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a\right]

maps functions with zero average in functions with zero average,
and
\Pi_{0}^{\bot}\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a\right]=\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a\right]\Pi_{0}^{\bot}
.

\bullet

If
a
is a homogeneous symbol,
the two definitions of quantization in (
2.17
) differ
by a smoothing operator according to
Definition
2.17
below.

\bullet

Definition
2.10

is independent of the cut-off functions
\psi_{p}
,
\psi
,
up to smoothing operators (Definition
2.17
).

\bullet

The action of

\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a\right]
on the spaces
H^{s}_{0}
only depends
on the values of the symbol
a(u;t,x,\xi)

for
|\xi|\geq 1
.
Therefore, we may identify two symbols
a(u;t,x,\xi)
and

b(u;t,x,\xi)
if they agree for
|\xi|\geq 1/2
.
In particular, whenever we encounter a symbol that is not smooth at
\xi=0
,
such as, for example,
a=g(x)|\xi|^{m}
for
m\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, or
{\rm sign}(\xi)
,
we will consider its smoothed out version

\chi(\xi)a
, where

\chi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
is an even and positive cut-off function satisfying

\chi(\xi)=0\;\;{\rm if}\;\;|\xi|\leq\tfrac{1}{8}\,,\quad\chi(\xi)=1\;\;{\rm if}\;\;|\xi|>\tfrac{1}{4}\,,\quad\partial_{\xi}\chi(\xi)>0\quad\forall\xi\in\big(\tfrac{1}{8},\tfrac{1}{4}\big)\,.

(2.18)

Remark 2.12
.

Given a paradifferential operator

A=\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a(x,\xi)\right]
it results

\overline{A}=\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[\overline{a(x,-\xi)}\right]\,,\quad A^{\intercal}=\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a(x,-\xi)\right]\,,\quad A^{*}=\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[\overline{a(x,\xi)}\right]\,,

(2.19)

where
A^{\intercal}
is the transposed operator with respect to the real scalar product

\langle u,v\rangle_{r}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)\,{v(x)}\,{\rm d}x
, and

A^{*}
denotes the adjoint operator with respect to the complex
scalar product of
L^{2}_{0}
in (
2.4
). It results
A^{*}=\overline{A}^{\intercal}
.

\bullet

A paradifferential operator
A=\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a(x,\xi)\right]
is
real
(i.e.
A=\overline{A}
) if

\overline{a(x,\xi)}=a(x,-\xi)\,.

(2.20)

It is
symmetric
(i.e.
A=A^{\intercal}
)
if
a(x,\xi)=a(x,-\xi)
.
A operator
\partial_{x}\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a(x,\xi)\right]
is Hamiltonian if and only if

a(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}\qquad\text{and}\qquad a(x,\xi)=a(x,-\xi)\quad\text{is \ even \ in \ }\xi\,.

(2.21)

We now provide the action of a paradifferential operator on Sobolev spaces, cf.
[
5
, Prop. 3.8]
.

Lemma 2.13

(Action of a paradifferential operator)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
.

i)

If
p\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
s_{0}>0
such that for any symbol
a
in
{\tilde{\Gamma}^{m}_{p}}
,
there is a constant
C>0
, depending only on
s
and on (
2.7
)
with
\gamma=\beta=0
,
such that, for any
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{p})
, for
p\geq 1
,

\left\|\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a(u_{1},\ldots,u_{p};\cdot)\right]u_{p+1}\right\|_{H^{s-m}_{0}}\leq C\left\|u_{1}\right\|_{H^{s_{0}}_{0}}\cdots\left\|u_{p}\right\|_{H^{s_{0}}_{0}}\left\|u_{p+1}\right\|_{H^{s}_{0}}\,.

If
p=0

the above bound holds replacing the right hand side with

C\left\|u_{p+1}\right\|_{H^{s}_{0}}
.

ii)

Let
\epsilon_{0}>0
,
p\in\mathbb{N}
,
K^{\prime}\leq K\in\mathbb{N}
,
a
in
{{\Gamma}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0}]}
.
There is
s_{0}>0
,
and a constant
C
, depending only on
s
,
\epsilon_{0}
, and on (
2.9
) with
0\leq\gamma\leq 2,\beta=0
,
such that, for any
t
in
I
, any
0\leq k\leq K-K^{\prime}
, any
u
in
{B^{K}_{{s_{0}}}(I;\epsilon_{0})}
,

\left\|\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[\partial_{t}^{k}a(u;t,\cdot)\right]\right\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(H^{s}_{0},H^{s-m}_{0})}\leq C\|u(t,\cdot)\|_{k+K^{\prime},s_{0}}^{p}\,,

so that

\|\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a(u;t,\cdot)\right]v(t)\|_{K-K^{\prime},s-m}\leq C\|u(t,\cdot)\|_{K,s_{0}}^{p}\|v(t)\|_{K-K^{\prime},s}
.

Classes of
m
-Operators and smoothing Operators.

Given integers
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{p+1})\in\mathbb{N}^{p+1}
, we denote by
\max_{2}(n_{1},\ldots,n_{p+1})

the second largest among
n_{1},\ldots,n_{p+1}
.

We now define
m
-operators.
The class
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{m}_{p}
denotes multilinear
operators that lose
m
derivatives
and are
p
-homogeneous in
u
,
while the class
\mathcal{M}_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}
contains non-homogeneous
operators which lose
m
derivatives,
vanish at degree at least
p
in
u
, satisfy tame estimates
and are
(K-K^{\prime})
-times differentiable in
t
.
The constant
\mu
in (
2.23
) takes into account possible loss of derivatives in
the “low" frequencies. The following definition is a small
adaptation of
[
10
, Def. 2.5]
as it defines
m
-operators acting
on
H^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C})

and not
\dot{H}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}^{2})
(and we state it directly in Fourier
series representation).

Definition 2.14

( Classes of
m
-operators)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
,
p,N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
K^{\prime}\leq K
, and
\epsilon_{0}>0
.

i)

p
-homogeneous
m
-operators.

We denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{m}_{p}

the space of
(p+1)
-linear translation invariant operators from

\left(H^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}\right)\right)^{p}\times H^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}\right)
to

H^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}\right)
, symmetric in
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{p})
,
with Fourier expansion

M(u)v\vcentcolon=M\left(u,\ldots,u\right)v=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(j_{1},\ldots,j_{p},j,k)\in\mathbb{Z}^{p+2}\\
j_{1}+\ldots+j_{p}+j=k\end{subarray}}M_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{p},j,k}\ u_{j_{1}}\ldots u_{j_{p}}v_{j}e^{{\rm i}kx}\,,

(2.22)

with coefficients
M_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{p},j,k}
symmetric in
j_{1},\ldots,j_{p}
,
satisfying the following: there are
\mu\geq 0
,
C>0
such that, for any

j_{1},\ldots,j_{p},j,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{p+2}
, it results

\left|M_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{p},j,k}\right|\leq C\ {\rm max}_{2}\left\{\left\langle j_{1}\right\rangle,\ldots,\left\langle j_{p}\right\rangle,\left\langle j\right\rangle\right\}^{\mu}\ \max\left\{\left\langle j_{1}\right\rangle,\ldots,\left\langle j_{p}\right\rangle,\left\langle j\right\rangle\right\}^{m}\,,

(2.23)

and the reality condition holds:

\overline{M_{\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,k}}=M_{-\vec{\jmath}_{p},-j,-k}\,,\qquad\forall\vec{\jmath}_{p}=\left(j_{1},\ldots,j_{p}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{p},\left(j,k\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\,.

(2.24)

If
p=0
the right hand side of (
2.22
) must be substituted with
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}M_{j}v_{j}e^{{\rm i}jx}
with
\left|M_{j}\right|\leq C\left\langle j\right\rangle^{m}
.

ii)

Non-homogeneous
m
-operators.

We denote by
\mathcal{M}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0}]

the space of operators
(u,t,v)\mapsto M(u;t)v
defined on
B_{C^{K^{\prime}}_{*}(I,H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}))}\left(0;\epsilon_{0}\right)\times I\times C^{0}_{*}(I,H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}))
for some
s_{0}>0
,
which are linear in the variable
v
and such that the following holds true.
For any
s\geq s_{0}
there are
C>0
and

\epsilon_{0}(s)\in]0,\epsilon_{0}[
such that for any

u\in B_{C^{K^{\prime}}_{*}(I,H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}))}\left(0;\epsilon_{0}\right)\cap C^{K}_{*}(I,H^{s}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}))
,
any
v\in C^{K-K^{\prime}}_{*}(I,H^{s}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}))
, any
0\leq k\leq K-K^{\prime}
,
t\in I
, we have that

\left\|{\partial_{t}^{k}\left(M(u;t)v\right)}\right\|_{s-\alpha k-m}\leq C\sum_{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}=k}\left(\|{v}\|_{k^{\prime\prime},s}\|{u}\|_{k^{\prime}+K^{\prime},{s_{0}}}^{p}+\|{v}\|_{k^{\prime\prime},{s_{0}}}\|u\|_{k^{\prime}+K^{\prime},{s_{0}}}^{p-1}\|u\|_{k^{\prime}+K^{\prime},s}\right)\,.

(2.25)

In case
p=0
we require the estimate

\|{\partial_{t}^{k}\left(M(u;t)v\right)}\|_{s-\alpha k-m}\leq C\|v\|_{k,s}
.
We say that a non-homogeneous
m
-operator
M\left(u;t\right)
is
real
if it is real valued for any

u\in B_{C^{K^{\prime}}_{*}(I,H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))}\left(0;\epsilon_{0}\right)
.

iii)

m
-Operators.

We denote by
\Sigma\mathcal{M}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]

the space of operators

M(u;t)v=\sum_{q=p}^{N}M_{q}(u,\ldots,u)v+M_{>N}(u;t)v

(2.26)

where
M_{q}
are homogeneous
m
-operators in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{m}_{q}
,
q=p,\ldots,N
and

M_{>N}
is a non–homogeneous
m
-operator
in
\mathcal{M}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
. We say that a
m
-operator
M\left(u;t\right)
is
real
if it is real valued for any

u\in B_{C^{K^{\prime}}_{*}(I,H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{R}))}\left(0;\epsilon_{0}\right)
.

iv)

Pluri-homogeneous
m
-Operator.

We denote
by
\Sigma_{p}^{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{m}_{q}
the
pluri-homogeneous
m
-operators of the form (
2.26
)
with
M_{>N}=0
.

We denote with
\dot{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}^{m}_{p},\ \dot{\mathcal{M}}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}\left[\epsilon_{0}\right]
and
\Sigma\dot{\mathcal{M}}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
the subspaces of
m
-operators
in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}^{m}_{p}
, respectively
\mathcal{M}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}\left[\epsilon_{0}\right]
and
\Sigma\mathcal{M}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
,
defined on zero-average functions
taking value
M(u)v
in zero-average functions.

Remark 2.15
.

By
[
10
, Lemma 2.8]
,
if
M(u_{1},\dots,u_{p})
is a
p
–homogeneous

m
-operator in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{m}
then

M(u)=M(u,\ldots,u)
is a non-homogeneous
m
-operator in

{\cal M}^{m}_{K,0,p}[\epsilon_{0}]
for any
\epsilon_{0}>0
and
K\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
We shall say that
M(u)
is in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{m}
.

Remark 2.16
.

The multiplication operator
v\mapsto\frac{1}{1+2f}v

belongs to
\Sigma\mathcal{M}^{0}_{K,0,0}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
.

If
m\leq 0
the operators in
\Sigma\mathcal{M}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]
are referred to as smoothing operators.

Definition 2.17

(Smoothing operators)
.

Let
\rho\geq 0
. A
(-\rho)
-operator
R(u)
belonging to
\Sigma\mathcal{M}^{-\rho}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]

is called a smoothing operator.
We also denote

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\rho}_{p}\vcentcolon=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{-\rho}_{p}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{-\rho}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0}]\vcentcolon=\mathcal{M}^{-\rho}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0}]\,,

\displaystyle\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\rho}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]\vcentcolon=\Sigma\mathcal{M}^{-\rho}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]\,.

We define
\dot{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\rho}_{p}=\dot{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}}^{-\rho}_{p}
,

\dot{\mathcal{R}}^{-\rho}_{K,K^{\prime},p}\left[\epsilon_{0}\right]=\dot{\mathcal{M}}^{-\rho}_{K,K^{\prime},p}\left[\epsilon_{0}\right]
and

\Sigma\dot{\mathcal{R}}^{-\rho}_{K,K^{\prime},p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]=\Sigma\dot{\mathcal{M}}^{-\rho}_{K,K^{\prime},p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
as in
Definition

2.14
.

If
R(u)
is a homogenous smoothing operator in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{\,-\rho}_{p}

then
\Pi_{0}^{\bot}R(u)
, where
\Pi_{0}^{\bot}
is defined
(
2.3
), restricted to zero average functions

u
, belongs to
\dot{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\rho}_{p}
.

Remark 2.18
.

\bullet

Lemma
2.13
implies that, if
a(u;t,\cdot)
is in
\Sigma\Gamma^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
,

m\in\mathbb{R}
, then
\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a(u;t,\cdot)\right]
defines a
m
-operator in

\Sigma\mathcal{M}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
.

\bullet

The composition of smoothing operators
R_{1}\in{{\Sigma{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\rho}_{K,K^{\prime},p_{1}}[\epsilon_{0},N]}

and
R_{2}\in{{\Sigma{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\rho}_{K,K^{\prime},p_{2}}[\epsilon_{0},N]}
is a smoothing operator
R_{1}R_{2}
in

{{\Sigma{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\rho}_{K,K^{\prime},p_{1}+p_{2}}[\epsilon_{0},N]}
. This is a particular case of Proposition
2.23
-(
i)
.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
,
\epsilon_{0}>0
,
K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
K^{\prime}\leq K
,
N,p\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

u\in B^{K}_{s,\mathbb{R}}\left(I;\epsilon_{0}\right)

and
M(u;t)
be a real operator in
\Sigma\mathcal{M}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
. Then

M(u;t)u
is a real function in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,K^{\prime},p+1}\left[\epsilon_{0},N+1\right]
according to
Definition

2.7
.

Proof.

We decompose
M(u;t)=\sum_{q=p}^{N}M_{q}(u)+M_{>N}(u;t)

in the usual homogeneous and non-homogeneous components.
We assume
u
is in
B^{K}_{s,\mathbb{R}}\left(I;\epsilon_{0}\right)
so that
u
has zero average.
We now prove that
M_{q}(u)u
is a function in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{q+1}
.
For any zero average function
u
, according
to (
2.22
) we have

(M_{q}(u)u)\left(x\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(j_{1},\ldots,j_{q},j)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}\setminus\left\{0\right\}\right)^{q+1}\\
j_{1}+\ldots+j_{p}+j=k\end{subarray}}M_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{p},j,k}\ u_{j_{1}}\ldots u_{j_{q}}u_{j}\ e^{{\rm i}\left(j_{1}+\ldots+j_{q}+j\right)x}.

Moreover, by (
2.23
), for any

(j_{1},\ldots,j_{p},j)=\left(\vec{\jmath}_{q},j\right)\in\left(\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\right)^{q+1}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|M_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{p},j,k}\right|\lesssim

\displaystyle\ {\rm max}_{2}\left\{\left\langle j_{1}\right\rangle,\ldots,\left\langle j_{q}\right\rangle,\left\langle j\right\rangle\right\}^{\mu}{\rm max}\left\{\left\langle j_{1}\right\rangle,\ldots,\left\langle j_{q}\right\rangle,\left\langle j\right\rangle\right\}^{m}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\ {\rm max}\left\{\left\langle j_{1}\right\rangle,\ldots,\left\langle j_{q}\right\rangle,\left\langle j\right\rangle\right\}^{2\max\left\{\mu,m\right\}}\lesssim\ \left|\left(\vec{\jmath}_{q},j\right)\right|^{2\max\left\{\mu,m\right\}}\,,

and, in view of (
2.11
),
we thus obtain that
M_{q}(u)u
is a function in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{q+1}
. In view of (
2.24
) the function
M_{q}(u)u
is real.

We now prove that
\left(M_{>N}\left(u;t\right)u\right)(t,x)
is a function in

\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,K^{\prime},N+2}\left(\epsilon_{0}\right)
.
Let
s_{0}\vcentcolon=1+\alpha\left(K-K^{\prime}\right)+m
.
For any
0\leq k\leq K-K^{\prime}
, for any
s\geq s_{0}
, and
0\leq\gamma\leq s-s_{0}
we have that

s-\alpha k-m>\gamma+1
, and

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{t}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\gamma}(M_{>N}\left(u;t\right)u)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\partial_{t}^{k}(M_{>N}\left(u;t\right)u)\right\|_{\gamma+1}\leq\left\|\partial_{t}^{k}(M_{>N}\left(u;t\right)u)\right\|_{s-\alpha k-m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{piove}}}{{\lesssim}}\left\|u\right\|_{k+K^{\prime},s_{0}}^{N+1}\left\|u\right\|_{k+K^{\prime},s}

proving, in view of

Definitions

2.2
and
2.7
,
that
M_{>N}\left(u;t\right)u
is a function in

\mathcal{F}_{K,K^{\prime},N+2}\left(\epsilon_{0}\right)
. The reality condition is verified since
M_{>N}
is a real
m
-operator per hypothesis.
∎

Symbolic calculus.

Let

\sigma(D_{x},D_{\xi},D_{y},D_{\eta})\vcentcolon=D_{\xi}D_{y}-D_{x}D_{\eta}

where
D_{x}\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{{\rm i}}\partial_{x}
and
D_{\xi},D_{y},D_{\eta}
are similarly defined.
The following is Definition 3.11 in
[
5
]
.

Definition 2.20

(Asymptotic expansion of composition symbol)
.

Let
p
,
p^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
K^{\prime}\leq K
,
\rho\geq 0
,
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\epsilon_{0}>0
.
Consider symbols
a\in\Sigma\Gamma_{K,K^{\prime},p}^{m}[\epsilon_{0},N]
and
b\in\Sigma\Gamma^{m^{\prime}}_{K,K^{\prime},p^{\prime}}[\epsilon_{0},N]
. For
u
in
B_{\sigma}^{K}(I;\epsilon_{0})

we define, for
\rho<\sigma-s_{0}
, the symbol

(a\#_{\rho}b)\left(u;t,x,\xi\right)\vcentcolon=\sum_{k=0}^{\rho}\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{{\rm i}}{2}\sigma\left(D_{x},D_{\xi},D_{y},D_{\eta}\right)\right)^{k}\Big[a(u;t,x,\xi)b(u;t,y,\eta)\Big]_{|_{\begin{subarray}{c}x=y,\xi=\eta\end{subarray}}}

(2.27)

modulo symbols in
\Sigma\Gamma^{m+m^{\prime}-\rho}_{K,K^{\prime},p+p^{\prime}}[\epsilon_{0},N]
.

The symbol
a\#_{\rho}b
belongs to
\Sigma\Gamma^{m+m^{\prime}}_{K,K^{\prime},p+p^{\prime}}[\epsilon_{0},N]
.
Moreover

a\#_{\rho}b=ab+\frac{1}{2{\rm i}}\{a,b\}

(2.28)

up to a symbol in
\Sigma\Gamma^{m+m^{\prime}-2}_{K,K^{\prime},p+p^{\prime}}[\epsilon_{0},N]
,
where

\{a,b\}\vcentcolon=\partial_{\xi}a\ \partial_{x}b-\partial_{x}a\ \partial_{\xi}b

denotes the Poisson bracket.

The following result is proved in Proposition
3.12
in
[
5
]
.

Proposition 2.21

(Composition of Bony-Weyl operators)
.

Let
p,q,N,K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
K^{\prime}\leq K
,
\rho\geq 0
,
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\epsilon_{0}>0
.
Consider symbols

a\in\Sigma{\Gamma}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]
and
b\in\Sigma{\Gamma}^{m^{\prime}}_{K,K^{\prime},q}[\epsilon_{0},N]
.
Then

\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a(u;t,x,\xi)\right]\circ\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[b(u;t,x,\xi)\right]-\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[(a\#_{\rho}b)(u;t,x,\xi)\right]

(2.29)

is a smoothing operator in
\Sigma\dot{\mathcal{R}}^{-\rho+m+m^{\prime}}_{K,K^{\prime},p+q}[\epsilon_{0},N]
.

We have the following result, see e.g. Lemma 7.2 in
[
5
]
.

Lemma 2.22

(Bony paraproduct decomposition)
.

Let
u_{1},u_{2}
be functions in
H^{\sigma}(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C})
with

\sigma>\frac{1}{2}
. Then

u_{1}u_{2}=\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[u_{1}\right]u_{2}+\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[u_{2}\right]u_{1}+R_{1}(u_{1})u_{2}+R_{2}(u_{2})u_{1}

(2.30)

where for
\mathsf{j}=1,2
,
R_{\mathsf{j}}
is a homogeneous smoothing operator in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\rho}_{1}
for any
\rho\geq 0
.

We now state other composition results for
m
-operators which follow as in

[
10
, Proposition 2.15]
.

Proposition 2.23

(Compositions of
m
-operators)
.

Let
p,p^{\prime},N,K,K^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
K^{\prime}\leq K
and
\epsilon_{0}>0
. Let
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then

i)

If
M(u;t)
is in

\Sigma\mathcal{M}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]
and
M^{\prime}(u;t)
is in

\Sigma\mathcal{M}^{m^{\prime}}_{K,K^{\prime},p^{\prime}}[\epsilon_{0},N]
then the composition

M(u;t)\circ M^{\prime}(u;t)

is in
\Sigma\mathcal{M}^{m+\max(m^{\prime},0)}_{K,K^{\prime},p+p^{\prime}}[\epsilon_{0},N]
.

ii)

If
M(u)
is a homogeneous
m
-operator in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{m}

and
M^{(\ell)}(u;t)
,
\ell=1,\dots,p+1
, are matrices of
m_{\ell}
-operators in

\Sigma\mathcal{M}^{m_{\ell}}_{K,K^{\prime},q_{\ell}}[\epsilon_{0},N]
with
m_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}
,

q_{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
then

M\left(M^{(1)}(u;t)u,\ldots,M^{(p)}(u;t)u\right)M^{(p+1)}(u;t)

belongs to
\Sigma\mathcal{M}_{K,K^{\prime},p+\bar{q}}^{m+\bar{m}}[\epsilon_{0},N]
with
\bar{m}\vcentcolon=\sum_{\ell=1}^{p+1}\max(m_{\ell},0)
and
\bar{q}\vcentcolon=\sum_{\ell=1}^{p+1}q_{\ell}
.

iii)

If
M(u;t)
is in
{\mathcal{M}}_{K,0,p}^{m}[\breve{\epsilon}_{0}]
for any
\breve{\epsilon}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
\mathbb{M}_{0}(u;t)
belongs to
\mathcal{M}^{0}_{K,K^{\prime},0}[\epsilon_{0}]
,
then
M(\mathbb{M}_{0}(u;t)u;t)
is in
\mathcal{M}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0}]
.

iv)

Let
a
be a symbol in
{{\Sigma\Gamma}^{m}_{K,K^{\prime},p}[\epsilon_{0},N]}
with
m\geq 0
and
R
a smoothing operator in
{{\Sigma{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\rho}_{K,K^{\prime},p^{\prime}}[\epsilon_{0},N]}
. Then

\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a(u;t,\cdot)\right]\circ R(u;t)\,,\quad R(u;t)\circ\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a(u;t,\cdot)\right]\,,

are in
{{\Sigma{\mathcal{R}}}^{-\rho+m}_{K,K^{\prime},p+p^{\prime}}[\epsilon_{0},N]}
.

Notation 2.24
.

In the sequel if
K^{\prime}=0
we denote a
symbol
a(u;t,x,\xi)
in
\Gamma_{K,0,p}^{m}[\epsilon_{0}]
simply as

a(u;x,\xi)
,
and a smoothing operator in
R(u;t)

in

\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\rho}_{K,0,p}[\epsilon_{0},N]
simply as
R(u)
,
without writing the
t
-dependence.

We finally provide the Bony paralinearization formula
of the composition operator.

Lemma 2.25

(Bony Paralinearization formula)
.

Let
F
be a smooth
\mathbb{C}
-valued function defined on a neighborhood of zero in
\mathbb{C}
, vanishing at zero at order
q\in\mathbb{N}
. Then there is
\epsilon_{0}>0

and a smoothing operator
R\left(u\right)

in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\rho}_{K,0,q^{\prime}}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
,
q^{\prime}\vcentcolon=\max(q-1,1)
, for any
\rho
, such that

\displaystyle F(u)=\ \textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[F^{\prime}\left(u\right)\right]u+R\left(u\right)u\,.

(2.31)

Proof.

The formula
follows by combination of
[
5
, Lemmata 3.19 and 7.2]
.
∎

2.2
z
-dependent paradifferential calculus

Along the
paralinearization process of the
\alpha
-SQG equation
in Section
4
we
shall encounter parameter dependent paradifferential operators depending on a
2\pi
-periodic variable
z
.
The following “Kernel-functions"
have to be considered as Taylor remainders of maps of the form
F\left(u;x,z\right)
at
z=0

which are smooth in
u
and
with finite regularity in
x
and
z
. We are interested in the behavior of such
functions close to
z=0
.

Definition 2.26

(Kernel functions)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{R}
,
p,N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

K\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and
\epsilon_{0}>0
.

i)

p
-
homogeneous Kernel-functions.
If
p\in\mathbb{N}

we denote
\widetilde{K\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{p}
the space of

z
-dependent,
p
-homogeneous maps
from
H^{\infty}_{0}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}\right)
to the space of

x
-translation invariant real functions

\varrho(u;x,z)
of class
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}

in
(x,z)\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
with Fourier expansion

\varrho(u;x,z)=\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{p}\in{\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}}\!\varrho_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{p}}(z)u_{j_{1}}\cdots u_{j_{p}}e^{{\rm i}(j_{1}+\cdots+j_{p})x}\,,\quad z\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{0\}\,,

(2.32)

with coefficients
\varrho_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{p}}(z)
of class
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}\right)
,
symmetric in
(j_{1},\ldots,j_{p})
,
satisfying the reality condition

\overline{\varrho_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{p}}}\left(z\right)=\varrho_{-j_{1},\ldots,-j_{p}}\left(z\right)

and the
following: for any
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there exist
\mu>0
and
a constant
C>0
such that

\left|\partial_{z}^{l}\varrho_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{p}}(z)\right|\leq C\left|\vec{\jmath}\right|^{\mu}\ \left|z\right|^{n-l}_{\mathbb{T}}\,,\quad\forall\vec{\jmath}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{p})\in(\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\})^{p}\,.

(2.33)

For
p=0
we denote by
\widetilde{K\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{0}
the space of
maps
z\mapsto\varrho(z)
which satisfy

\left|\partial_{z}^{l}\varrho(z)\right|\leq C\,\left|z\right|^{n-l}_{\mathbb{T}}
.

ii)

Non-homogeneous Kernel-functions.
We denote by
K\mathcal{F}_{K,0,p}^{n}[\epsilon_{0}]
the space of
z
-dependent,
real functions
\varrho(u;x,z)
,
defined for
u\in B_{s_{0}}^{0}(I;\epsilon_{0})
for some
s_{0}
large enough, such that for any
0\leq k\leq K
and
l\leq\max\left\{0,\left\lceil 1+n\right\rceil\big.\right\}
, any
s\geq s_{0}
, there are
C>0
,
0<\epsilon_{0}(s)<\epsilon_{0}
and for any
u\in B_{s_{0}}^{K}\left(I;\epsilon_{0}(s)\right)\cap C_{*}^{k}\left(I,H_{0}^{s}\left(\mathbb{T};\mathbb{C}\right)\right)
and any
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, with
\gamma\leq s-s_{0},
one has the estimate

\left|\partial_{t}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\gamma}\partial_{z}^{l}\varrho\left(u;x,z\right)\right|\leq C\|u\|_{k,s_{0}}^{p-1}\|u\|_{k,s}\ \left|z\right|^{n-l}_{\mathbb{T}}\,,\qquad z\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{0\}\,.

(2.34)

If
p=0
the right hand side in (
2.34
)
has to be replaced by
\left|z\right|^{n-l}_{\mathbb{T}}
.

iii)

Kernel-functions.
We denote by
\Sigma K\mathcal{F}_{K,0,p}^{n}[\epsilon_{0},N]
the space of real functions
of the form

\varrho(u;x,z)=\sum_{q=p}^{N}\varrho_{q}\left(u;x,z\right)+\varrho_{>N}(u;x,z)

(2.35)

where
\varrho_{q}\left(u;x,z\right)
,
q=p,\dots,N

are homogeneous Kernel functions in
\widetilde{K\mathcal{F}}_{q}^{n}
, and

\varrho_{>N}(u;x,z)

is a non-homogeneous Kernel function in
K\mathcal{F}_{K,0,N+1}^{n}[\epsilon_{0}]
.

A Kernel function
\varrho(u;x,z)
is
real
if it is real valued for any

u\in B_{s_{0},\mathbb{R}}^{0}(I;\epsilon_{0})
.

In view of Remark
2.4
,
a homogeneous Kernel function
\varrho(u;x,z)
in
\widetilde{K\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{p}

defines a non-homogenous Kernel function in
K\mathcal{F}_{K,0,p}^{n}[\epsilon_{0}]

for any
\epsilon_{0}>0
.

Remark 2.27
.

Let
\varrho\left(u;x,z\right)
be
a Kernel function in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{n}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
with
n\geq 0
,
which admits a continuous extension in
z=0
. Then its trace

\varrho\left(u;x,0\right)
is a function in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
.

Remark 2.28
.

If
\varrho(u;x,z)
is a homogeneous Kernel function
\widetilde{K\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{p}
,
the two definitions of quantization in (
2.17
) differ
by a Kernel smoothing operator in
\widetilde{K\mathcal{R}}^{-\rho,n}_{p}
, for any
\rho>0
,
according to
Definition
2.33
below.

Remark 2.29
.

If
\varrho_{1}(u;x,z)
is a Kernel function in

\Sigma K\mathcal{F}^{n_{1}}_{K,0,p_{1}}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]

and
\varrho_{2}(u;x,z)
in

\Sigma K\mathcal{F}^{n_{2}}_{K,0,p_{2}}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
,
then the sum

(\varrho_{1}+\varrho_{2})(u;x,z)
is a Kernel function in
\Sigma K\mathcal{F}^{\min\left\{n_{1},n_{2}\right\}}_{K,0,\min\left\{p_{1},p_{2}\right\}}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
and the product

(\varrho_{1}\varrho_{2})(u;x,z)
is a Kernel function in

\Sigma K\mathcal{F}^{n_{1}+n_{2}}_{K,0,p_{1}+p_{2}}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
.

Remark 2.30
.

Let
\varrho\left(u;x,z\right)
be a Kernel function in
\Sigma K\mathcal{F}^{n}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
with
n>-1
. Then

\mathop{\,\hbox to0.0pt{--\hss}\!\!\!\int}\nolimits\varrho\left(u;x,z\right)\textnormal{d}z
is a function in

\Sigma\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{R}}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
.
This follows directly integrating (
2.33
) and (
2.34
) in
z
.

The
m
-Kernel-operators defined below are a
z
-dependent family of
m
-operators with coefficients small as
|z|^{n}_{\mathbb{T}}
. They appear for example as
smoothing operators in the composition of Bony-Weyl quantizations of
Kernel-functions.

Definition 2.31
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{R}
,
p,N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

K\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
\epsilon_{0}>0
.

i)

p
-homogeneous
m
-Kernel-operator.

We denote by
\widetilde{K\mathcal{M}}^{m,n}_{p}

the space of

z
-dependent,
x
-translation invariant homogeneous
m
-operators
according to
Definition

2.14
,
Item

i
, in which the constant

C
is substituted with
C\left|z\right|^{n}_{\mathbb{T}}
, equivalently

M(u;z)v\left(x\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,k)\in\mathbb{Z}^{p+2}\\
j_{1}+\ldots+j_{p}+j=k\end{subarray}}M_{\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,k}\left(z\right)u_{j_{1}}\ldots u_{j_{p}}v_{j}e^{{\rm i}kx}\,,\qquad z\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{0\}\,,

(2.36)

with coefficients satisfying

\big|M_{\vec{\jmath}_{p},j,k}\left(z\right)\big|\leq C\ {\rm max}_{2}\left\{\left\langle j_{1}\right\rangle,\ldots,\left\langle j_{p}\right\rangle,\left\langle j\right\rangle\right\}^{\mu}\ \max\left\{\left\langle j_{1}\right\rangle,\ldots,\left\langle j_{p}\right\rangle,\left\langle j\right\rangle\right\}^{m}\left|z\right|^{n}_{\mathbb{T}}\,.

(2.37)

If
p=0
the right hand side of (
2.36
) is replaced by

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}M_{j}\left(z\right)v_{j}e^{{\rm i}jx}
with
\big|M_{j}\left(z\right)\big|\leq C\left\langle j\right\rangle^{m}\left|z\right|^{n}_{\mathbb{T}}
.

ii)

Non-homogeneous
m
-Kernel-operator.

We denote by
K\mathcal{M}^{m,n}_{K,0,p}[\epsilon_{0}]

the space of
z
-dependent,
non-homogeneous operators
M(u;z)v
defined for any

z\in\mathbb{T}\setminus\{0\}
, such that for any
0\leq k\leq K

\left\|{\partial_{t}^{k}\left(M(u;z)v\right)}\right\|_{s-\alpha k-m}\\
\leq C\left|z\right|^{n}_{\mathbb{T}}\ \sum_{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}=k}\left(\|{v}\|_{k^{\prime\prime},s}\|{u}\|_{k^{\prime},{s_{0}}}^{p}+\|{v}\|_{k^{\prime\prime},{s_{0}}}\|u\|_{k^{\prime},{s_{0}}}^{p-1}\|u\|_{k^{\prime},s}\right)\,.

(2.38)

iii)

m
-Kernel-Operator.

We denote by
\Sigma K\mathcal{M}^{m,n}_{K,0,p}[\epsilon_{0},N]

the space of operators
of the form

M(u;z)v=\sum_{q=p}^{N}M_{q}(u,\ldots,u)v+M_{>N}(u;z)v

(2.39)

where
M_{q}
are homogeneous
m
-Kernel
operators in
\widetilde{K\mathcal{M}}^{m,n}_{q}
,
q=p,\ldots,N
and
M_{>N}
is a non–homogeneous
m
-Kernel-operator in

\mathcal{M}^{m,n}_{K,0,N+1}[\epsilon_{0}]
.

iv)

Pluri-homogeneous
m
-Kernel-Operator.
We denote
by
\Sigma_{p}^{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{m}_{q}
the
pluri-homogeneous
m
-operators of the form (
2.39
)
with
M_{>N}=0
.

Remark 2.32
.

Given
\varrho\left(u;x,z\right)\in\Sigma K\mathcal{F}^{n}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
then
\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[\varrho\left(u;x,z\right)\right]\in\Sigma K\mathcal{M}^{0,n}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
.

Definition 2.33

(Kernel-smoothing operators)
.

Given

\rho>0
we define the homogeneous and non-homogeneous Kernel-smoothing operators as

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}\mathcal{R}^{-\rho,n}_{p}\vcentcolon=\widetilde{K}\mathcal{M}^{-\rho,n}_{p},

\displaystyle K\mathcal{R}^{-\rho,n}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0}\right]\vcentcolon=K\mathcal{M}^{-\rho,n}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0}\right],

\displaystyle\Sigma K\mathcal{R}^{-\rho,n}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]\vcentcolon=\Sigma K\mathcal{M}^{-\rho,n}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right].

In view of
[
10
, Lemma 2.8]
,
if
M\left(u,\ldots,u;z\right)
is a homogenous

m
-Kernel operator in
\widetilde{K}\mathcal{M}^{m,n}_{p}
then
M\left(u,\ldots,u;z\right)

defines a non-homogenous
m
-Kernel operator in
K\mathcal{M}^{m,n}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0}\right]
for any
\epsilon_{0}>0
and
K\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Proposition 2.34

(Composition of
z
-dependent operators)
.

Let
m,n,m^{\prime},n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
, and integers

K,p,p^{\prime},N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
p,p^{\prime}\leq N
.

1.

Let
\varrho\left(u;x,z\right)\in\Sigma K\mathcal{F}^{n}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
and
\varrho^{\prime}\left(u;x,z\right)\in\Sigma K\mathcal{F}^{n^{\prime}}_{K,0,p^{\prime}}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]

be Kernel functions. Then

\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[\varrho\left(u;x,z\right)\right]\circ\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[\varrho^{\prime}\left(u;x,z\right)\right]=\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[\varrho\ \varrho^{\prime}\left(u;x,z\right)\right]+R\left(u;z\right)

where
R\left(u;z\right)
is a Kernel-smoothing operator in
\Sigma K\mathcal{R}^{-\rho,n+n^{\prime}}_{K,0,p+p^{\prime}}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
for any
\rho\geq 0
;

2.

Let
M\left(u;z\right)
be a
m
-operator in

\Sigma K\mathcal{M}^{m,n}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
and

M^{\prime}\left(u;z\right)
be an
m^{\prime}
-operator in

\Sigma K\mathcal{M}^{m^{\prime},n^{\prime}}_{K,0,p^{\prime}}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
. Then
M\left(u;z\right)\circ M^{\prime}\left(u;z\right)
belongs to

\Sigma K\mathcal{M}^{m+\max\left(m^{\prime},0\right),n+n^{\prime}}_{K,0,p+p^{\prime}}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
;

3.

Let
\varrho\left(u;x,z\right)
be a Kernel function in

\Sigma K\mathcal{F}^{n}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
and
R\left(u;z\right)

be a Kernel smoothing operator in
\Sigma K\mathcal{R}^{-\rho,n^{\prime}}_{K,0,p^{\prime}}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
then
\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[\varrho\left(u;x,z\right)\right]\circ R\left(u;z\right)
and
R\left(u;z\right)\circ\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[\varrho\left(u;x,z\right)\right]
are a Kernel smoothing operator in
\Sigma K\mathcal{R}^{-\rho,n+n^{\prime}}_{K,0,p+p^{\prime}}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
;

4.

Let
M\left(u;z\right)
be an homogeneous
m
-Kernel operator
in
\widetilde{K}\mathcal{M}^{m,n}_{1}
, and
M^{\prime}\left(u;z\right)
in
\Sigma K\mathcal{M}^{0,0}_{K,0,0}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
then
M\left(M^{\prime}\left(u;z\right)u;z\right)\in\Sigma K\mathcal{M}^{m,n}_{K,0,1}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
.

Proof.

The proof of item
1

is performed in
[
5
, Proposition 3.12]
keeping track of the dependence in the variable
z
of the symbols as in (
2.33
),
(
2.34
) when
\gamma=0
.
More precisely
\varrho
and
\varrho^{\prime}
satisfy
z
-dependent inequalities (cf. (
2.7
), (
2.9
))

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\varrho_{q}\left(\Pi_{\vec{n}}\mathcal{U};x,z\right)\right|\leq\ C\left|z\right|^{n}_{\mathbb{T}}\left|\vec{n}\right|^{\mu+\alpha}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left\|\Pi_{n_{j}}u_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{t}^{k}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\varrho\left(u;x,z\right)\right|\leq\ C\left|z\right|^{n}_{\mathbb{T}}\left\|u\right\|_{k,s_{0}}^{p-1}\left\|u\right\|_{k,s}\,,

and, in the proof of
[
5
, Proposition 3.12]
, the seminorm of the composed symbol always appear as a product of the seminorms of the factor symbols.
The proof of item
2
is the same as in
[
10
, Proposition 2.15-i]
, keeping track of the dependence in
z
of the
m
-operators.
For
item
3
, see
[
10
, Proposition 2.19-i]
factoring
the dependence on
z
.
Item

4
is a consequence of
[
10
, Proposition 2.15-ii]
factoring
the dependence on
z
.
∎

Finally integrating (
2.37
) and (
2.38
) in
z
we deduce the following lemma.

Lemma 2.35
.

Let
R\left(u;z\right)
be a Kernel smoothing operator in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\rho,n}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]

with
n>-1
.
Then

\mathop{\,\hbox to0.0pt{--\hss}\!\!\!\int}\nolimits R\left(u;z\right)g\left(x-z\right)\textnormal{d}z=R_{1}\left(u\right)g\,,\qquad\mathop{\,\hbox to0.0pt{--\hss}\!\!\!\int}\nolimits R\left(u;z\right)\textnormal{d}z=R_{2}\left(u\right)\,,

where
R_{1}\left(u\right)
,
R_{2}\left(u\right)
are smoothing operators
in
\Sigma\mathcal{R}^{-\rho}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
.

The following proposition will be crucial in Section
4
.

Proposition 2.36
.

Let
n>-1
and
\varrho\left(u;x,z\right)
be a Kernel-function in

\Sigma K\mathcal{F}^{n}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
.
Let us define the operator, for any
g\in H^{s}_{0}(\mathbb{T})
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

\left(\mathcal{T}_{\varrho}g\right)\left(x\right)\vcentcolon=\mathop{\,\hbox to0.0pt{--\hss}\!\!\!\int}\nolimits\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[\varrho\left(u;\bullet,z\right)\right]g\left(x-z\right)\textnormal{d}z\,.

(2.40)

Then there exists

•

a symbol
a\left(u;x,\xi\right)

in
\Sigma\Gamma^{-\left(1+n\right)}_{K,0,p}\left[\epsilon_{0},N\right]
satisfying (
2.20
);

•

a pluri-homogeneous smoothing operator
R\left(u\right)

in
\Sigma_{p}^{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\rho}_{q}

for any
\rho>0
;

such that
\mathcal{T}_{\varrho}g=\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[a\left(u;x,\xi\right)\right]g+R\left(u\right)g
.

Proof.

In view of Definition
2.10
and
2.28
we have that

\textnormal{Op}^{BW}\left[\varrho\left(u;x,z\right)\right]-\textnormal{Op}^{W}\left[\varrho_{\psi}\left(u;x,z\right)\right]=:R\left(u;z\right)

(2.41)

is a pluri-homogeneous Kernel smoothing operator in

\Sigma_{p}^{N}\widetilde{K\mathcal{R}}^{-\rho,n}_{q}
for any
\rho
.
Since
n>-1
, integrating in
z
,
we deduce that
\mathop{\,\hbox to0.0pt{--\hss}\!\!\!\int}\nolimits R\left(u;z\right)g(x-z)\textnormal{d}z=R\left(u\right)g
where

R\left(u\right)

is a pluri-homogeneous smoothing operator
in
\Sigma_{p}^{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{-\rho}_{q}
(cf.

Lemma

2.35
).

In view of (
2.17
) and (
2.16
) we compute for any
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}

\mathcal{F}_{x\to\nu}\left(\mathop{\,\hbox to0.0pt{--\hss}\!\!\!\int}\nolimits\textnormal{Op}^{W}\left[\varrho_{\psi}\left(u;x,z\right)\right]g\left(x-z\right)\textnormal{d}z\right)\left(\nu\right)\\
=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\psi\left(\nu-k,\frac{\nu+k}{2}\right)\mathop{\,\hbox to0.0pt{--\hss}\!\!\!\int}\nolimits\hat{\varrho}\left(u;\nu-k,z\right)e^{-{\rm i}kz}\textnormal{d}z\ \hat{g}\left(k\right)

where
\psi(\xi^{\prime},\xi)
is an admissible cut-off function, namely satisfying
(
2.14
)-(
2.15
).
Introducing another admissible cut-off function
\tilde{\psi}(\xi^{\prime},\xi)

identically equal to one on the support of

\psi(\xi^{\prime},\xi)
, and since
\widehat{g}(0)=0
,

\mathcal{F}_{x\to\nu}\left(\mathop{\,\hbox to0.0pt{--\hss}\!\!\!\int}\nolimits\textnormal{Op}^{W}\left[\varrho_{\psi}\left(u;x,z\right)\right]\textnormal{d}z\right)\left(\nu\right)\\
=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\psi\left(\nu-k,\frac{\nu+k}{2}\right)\tilde{\psi}\left(\nu-k,\frac{\nu+k}{2}\right)\chi\left(2k\right)\mathop{\,\hbox to0.0pt{--\hss}\!\!\!\int}\nolimits\hat{\varrho}\left(u;\nu-k,z\right)e^{-{\rm i}kz}\textnormal{d}z\ \hat{g}\left(k\right)

(2.42)

where
\chi(\cdot)
is the
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
function defined
in (
2.18
).
Introducing a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
function
\eta:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
with compact support
such that

\displaystyle\eta(z)=1\,,\ \forall|z|\leq\frac{\pi}{2}\,,

\displaystyle\eta(z)=0\,,\ \forall|z|\geq\frac{3\pi}{2}\,,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\eta(z+2\pi j)=1\,,\ \forall z\in\mathbb{R}\,,

(2.43)

we may write the integral on
\mathbb{T}
as
